is the class of the 2-cocycle

\displaystyle y:G_{\O}(K\overline{k})\times G_{\O}(K\overline{k})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mu_{n}

\displaystyle(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle\sigma(s(x(\tau)))s(x(\sigma))s(x(\tau\sigma))^{-1}=\frac{t(\sigma)t(\tau)(\sqrt[n]{b})}{t(\sigma\tau)(\sqrt[n]{b})}.

The two 2-cocycles
z
and
y
are the same because
y(\sigma,\tau)=t(\sigma\tau)(z(\sigma,\tau))
equals
z(\sigma,\tau)
as

is fixed by

.
Therefore, we proved that (
2.24
) commutes, which implies that
tg\circ f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(1_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
corresponds to the element of
H^{2}_{\et}(X_{\overline{k}},\mu_{n})
of
\tr_{X,n}
-trace 1.

Recall that
\Omega_{K}
denotes the prime-to-

completion of the étale fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{\et}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\overline{k}}-\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}\})
where

. So the prime-to-

is a quotient of

.
Let

denote the surjection defined in Lemma
2.9
, and let
\alpha:G^{\prime}_{S}(K)\to\widetilde{H}
that factors through the map obtained by restricting

to

and then taking the prime-to-

completion, we obtain the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}_{S}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\varpi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}

which induces a commutative diagram of the Hochschild–Serre exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(N,\mu_{n})^{G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\ker\varpi,\mu_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{n})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tg_{\varpi,n}}

(2.25)

Here the isomorphism in the second row follows from Lemma
2.10
.

We use the notation in Lemma
2.9
to let
\overline{g}_{i}
denote the image of
g_{i}
in
\Omega_{K}
for each
i=1,\ldots,m
.
By Lemma
2.9
,
\varrho_{K}
identifies
\ker\varpi
as the Galois group of an extension of
K^{\prime}_{\O}
, denoted by
E/K^{\prime}_{\O}
, that is ramified only at the primes over the prime
v
of
\overline{k}(t)
corresponding to
P_{m}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{p}}_{c}
denote all the primes of
K\overline{k}
lying above
v
, and let
e
denote the ramification index of the extension
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}/v
.
Let
h
denote
\varrho_{K}(\overline{g}_{m}^{e})
in
\widetilde{H}
.
Then
h
generates the inertia subgroup of
E/K^{\prime}_{\O}
at one prime above
v
, and because
h\in\ker\varpi=\ker\pi
is in the center of
\widetilde{G}
(where
\pi
and
\widetilde{G}
are defined in Lemma
2.4
), we have that
h
generates the inertia subgroup at each prime above
v
.

Let
I(\underline{g})_{n}
denote the image of
I(\underline{g})
under the surjection
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p)^{\prime}}\to\mu_{n}
.
For an element
\zeta\in\mu_{n}
, a pro-cyclic group
D
, and one generator
d
of
D
, we write
[d\mapsto\zeta]
for the element of
\Hom(D,\mu_{n})
that sends
d
to
\zeta
.
Then
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
is the element
[\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})\mapsto I(\underline{g})_{n}]
in
H^{1}(\ker\varpi,\mu_{n})
.
Because
\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})
is in the center of
\widetilde{G}
, it follows by Lemma
2.9
and
\ord(\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{m}))=e
that

\varrho_{K}(\overline{g}_{m}^{e})=\left(\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{m})\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})^{-1}\right)^{e}=\widetilde{\rho}_{K}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})^{-e},

and hence the image of
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
in the
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}
-compoment of
H^{1}(N,\mu_{n})^{G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)}=\oplus_{{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\mu_{n})
is
[\overline{g}_{m}^{e}\mapsto I(\underline{g})_{n}^{-e}]
. Recall that
I(\underline{g})_{n}
is defined to be
\frac{\overline{g}_{m}(\sqrt[n]{a_{v}})}{\sqrt[n]{a_{v}}}
for any uniformizer
a_{v}
of the ring of integers of
\overline{k}(t)_{v}
. So

\left[\overline{g}_{m}^{e}\mapsto I(\underline{g})_{n}^{-e}\right]=\left[\overline{g}_{m}^{e}\mapsto\left(\frac{\overline{g}_{m}^{e}(\sqrt[n]{a_{v}})}{\sqrt[n]{a_{v}}}\right)^{-1}\right]=\left[\overline{g}_{m}^{e}\mapsto\left(\frac{\overline{g}_{m}^{e}(\sqrt[n]{a_{\mathfrak{p}}})}{\sqrt[n]{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\right)^{-e}\right].

Here we choose
a_{v}
and
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
to be compatible uniformizers such that
a_{v}=a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{e}
, and then the last equality is because
(K\overline{k})_{\mathfrak{p}}/\overline{k}(t)_{v}
is a degree-
e
completely ramified extension.
So we see that the image of
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
under the composition map
H^{1}(\ker\varpi,\mu_{n})\to H^{1}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}},\mu_{n})\to H^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{n})
has trace
-e
. By summing over all
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}
and considering the diagram (
2.25
), we see that the image of
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
in
H^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{n})
has trace
-ec=-[K\overline{k}:\overline{k}(t)]=-|\Gamma|
. ∎

Corollary 2.12
.

The map
{\mathfrak{i}}
in (
2.21
) identifies (
2.18
) as a central extension of
G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)
with kernel
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
. Then the class of
H^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}})
corresponding to this central extension is

(1)

the image of
{\mathfrak{i}}
under the inverse limit
tg_{\varpi}:=\varprojlim_{n}tg_{\varpi,n}
of the transgression map described in (
2.20
); and

(2)

the image of
\epsilon
in Remark
2.7
under the restriction map induced by
G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)\hookrightarrow\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t))
.

In particular, this class corresponds to the class of
H_{\et}^{2}(X_{\overline{k}},\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}})
of trace
-|\Gamma|
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
denote a splitting of
\varpi
such that
\varphi(1)=1
. Consider the following 2-cocycle in
Z^{2}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\ker\pi)

\lambda:G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)\times G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)\to\ker\varpi

defined by
\lambda(x,y)=\varphi(xy)^{-1}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)
, which is the normalized 2-cocycle corresponding to the extension
\varpi
. Then the transgression map
tg_{\varpi}
maps
{\mathfrak{i}}
to the class of
{\mathfrak{i}}\circ\lambda

[
16
, Pages 16-17]
.
Since the class of
{\mathfrak{i}}\circ\lambda
is the normalized 2-cocycle corresponding to the central extension of
G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)
with kernel
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
described in the corollary, we proved the statement (
1
). The statement (
2
) is trivial, and the last statement follows by the statement (
1
) and Lemma
2.11
.
∎

2.3.
Definition of the invariant
\omega_{L/K}

Definition 2.13

(Invariant
\omega_{L/K}
associated to
L/K
)
.

For a subextension
L/K
of
K^{\prime}_{\O}/K
such that
L
is Galois over
k(t)
, we define
the
\omega
-invariant
\omega_{L/K}
associated to
L/K
to be the
\Frob_{k}
-equivairant homomorphism
\omega_{L/K}
that is the composition map as follows

\omega_{L/K}:\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\overset{\omega_{K}}{\longrightarrow}H_{2}(\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\overset{\coinf}{\longrightarrow}H_{2}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}

where the map
\coinf
is the coinflation map defined by the surjection
\alpha:\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t))\to\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
.
In particular, when
L=K^{\#}_{\O}
, we denote
\omega^{\#}_{K}:=\omega_{K^{\#}_{\O}/K}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
n
be an integer that is divisible by
|\Gamma|
, and
G,H
are two extensions of
\Gamma
such that
\ker(G\to\Gamma)
and
\ker(H\to\Gamma)
are pro-prime-to-
n
admissible
\Gamma
-groups. Assume that there is a surjection
\alpha:G\to H
and a
(n)^{\prime}
-Schur covering of
H

1\longrightarrow\ker\pi^{\prime}\longrightarrow S\overset{\pi^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}H\to 1.

(2.26)

Then there exists a
(n)^{\prime}
-Schur covering of
G
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

and the first vertical arrow is the composition map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(n)^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(H,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(n)^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\pi}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coinf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\pi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
d_{\pi}
and
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
is the differential maps defined by
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
respectively.

Proof.

There is a commutative diagram induced by
\alpha:G\to H

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(H,\ker\pi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(H_{2}(H,{\mathbb{Z}}),\ker\pi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G,\ker\pi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}),\ker\pi^{\prime}),}

where the vertical maps are induced by
\alpha
, and the horizontal maps are from the universal coefficient theorem. Moreover, since
\ker(G\to\Gamma)
and
\ker(H\to\Gamma)
are admissible
\Gamma
-groups, we have that
G^{ab}
and
H^{ab}
are both isomorphic to
\Gamma^{ab}
, so
\Ext^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(G,\ker\pi^{\prime})=\Ext^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(H,\ker\pi^{\prime})=0
and the two horizontal maps are isomorphisms.

The image of the class of
\pi^{\prime}
in the upper-right entry of the above diagram is the differential map
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
(we view
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
as the quotient map
H_{2}(H,{\mathbb{Z}})\to\ker\pi^{\prime}
that factors through
H_{2}(H,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(n)^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\ker\pi^{\prime}
). We let
d
denote the image of
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
in the lower-right entry, so
d
is the composition
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(n)^{\prime}}\to H_{2}(H,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(n)^{\prime}}\overset{d_{\pi^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}\ker\pi^{\prime}
.
Let
\overline{\pi}:E\to G
be a group extension of
G
whose corresponding class in
H^{2}(G,\ker\pi^{\prime})
is the preimage of
d
. Then
E
has a subgroup
E^{\prime}
such that
E^{\prime}\to G
is a stem extension of kernel isomorphic to
\im d
.
Because every stem extension is a homomorphic image of a Schur covering group
[
16
, Chap.11, Cor.2.4]
,
E^{\prime}\to G
can be extended to a
(n)^{\prime}
-Schur covering
\pi:T\to G
. Then one can check by our construction that
\pi
fits into the diagram in the lemma.
∎

The following is an analogue of Proposition
2.6
, which gives a group theoretical description of the invariant
\omega_{L/K}
.

Corollary 2.15
.

Use the assumptions and notation in Definition
2.13
. Let
\underline{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
be a system of inertia generators for
K
,
\varphi:\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\rangle\to\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
the surjection defined by
\underline{g}
and
L
, and

1\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow S\overset{\pi^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))\longrightarrow 1

be a
(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}
-Schur covering of
\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
. Then there is a unique surjection
\widetilde{\varphi}:\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\rangle\to S
such that
\varphi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\ord(\widetilde{\varphi}(g_{i}))=\ord(\varphi(g_{i}))
for each
i=1,\ldots,m
.

The invariant
\omega_{L/K}
is the composition of the map
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\to B
defined by
I(\underline{g})\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})
and the inverse of the differential map
H_{2}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}B
defined by
\pi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of
\widetilde{\varphi}
can be proven by repeating the proof of Proposition
2.6
(
1
).

Note that
G^{\prime}_{\O}(K)
is an admissible
\Gamma
-group, so is
\Gal(L\overline{k}/K\overline{k})
. By applying Lemma
2.14
to
\alpha:\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t))\twoheadrightarrow\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
, we obtain

\pi:\widetilde{G}\to\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t))
in the diagram in Lemma
2.14
. Then by Proposition
2.6
, for a choice of
\underline{g}
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

Then the corollary follows by the definitions of
\omega_{K}
and
\omega_{L/K}
, Proposition
2.6
(
3
), and Lemma
2.14
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

(1)

The invariant
\omega_{L/K}
is not necessarily an isomorphism, because the coinflation map is not.

(2)

Assume that
L
is contained in
K^{\#}_{\O}
. Then
\Frob_{k}
acts trivially on
\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
, and
\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
is naturally isomorphic to
\Gal(L/k(t))
. In this case, the invariant
\omega_{L/K}
factors through the prime-to-
p|\Gamma|
part of the roots of unity of
k
, which we denote by
\mu(k)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
. So by abuse of notation, we write

\omega_{L/K}:\mu(k)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\longrightarrow H_{2}(\Gal(L/k(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}.

The image of
w_{L/K}
is contained in the
|\mu(k)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}|
-torsion subgroup of
H_{2}(\Gal(L/k(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
.

(3)

Every argument in this section works when replacing “pro-prime-to-
(p|\Gamma|)
completion” with “pro-
\ell
completion”, for any prime
\ell
that does not divide
p|\Gamma|
. So we can define the pro-
\ell
analogues of the invariants
\omega_{K}
and
\omega_{L/K}
. Explicitly, under the assumptions in Definitions
2.8
and
2.13
, we can define
\Frob_{k}
-equivariant

\omega_{K,\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}H_{2}(\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})(\ell)\quad\text{and}

\omega_{L/K,\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)\longrightarrow H_{2}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})(\ell),

and give group theoretical descriptions of these invariants.

3.
Relationship between
\omega_{K}
and the Weil pairing associated to
K

Throughout this section, we assume that
k
,
K
,
p
and
\Gamma
be as in Notation
2.1
, and
\ell
is a prime such that
\ell\nmid p|\Gamma|
. In this section, we will discuss how the invariant
\omega_{K}
is abelianized to the Weil pairing associated to
K
. In §
3.1
, we will first explain how a pro-
\ell
presentation of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
determines its cup product. Then in §
3.2
, we will show how
\omega_{K}
determines the Weil pairing and how
\omega_{L/K}
determines the image of Weil pairing associated to
L/K
.

3.1.
Presentation of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
determines cup product

Because
\Gamma
is assumed to be non-trivial (see Notation
2.1
),
by
[
28
, Thm.10.1.2(i)(b) and (iii)]
,
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
is a pro-
\ell
Demuškin group generated by
2g
generators whose abelianization is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2g}
, and by a nice choice of the generating set, it admits a pro-
\ell
presentation in the form of

G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)\simeq\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{2g}\mid[x_{1},x_{2}][x_{3},x_{4}]\cdots[x_{2g-1},x_{2g}]=1\rangle.

Let
F
be a free pro-
\ell
group on
2g
generators
x_{1},\ldots,x_{2g}
and the following a pro-
\ell
presentation of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
(which is called a
minimal presentation
of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
because the minimal number of generators for
F
and
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
are equal)

1\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow F\overset{\phi}{\longrightarrow}G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)\longrightarrow 1.

(3.1)

Since
F
is free and
F^{ab}\simeq G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)^{ab}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2g}
, the transgression map in the Hochschild–Serre exact sequence of (
3.1
) is isomorphic, and we denote it by

tg:H^{1}(R,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})^{G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}).

Then for every element
\lambda
of
R
, there is a trace map
\tr_{\lambda}
associated to
\lambda
as follows

\displaystyle\tr_{\lambda}:H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle(tg^{-1}\varphi)(\lambda).

(3.2)

By definition of Demuškin groups and
[
28
, Prop.(3.9.12)(ii)(iii)]
, if
\lambda
generates
R
as a normal subgroup of
F
, then
\tr_{\lambda}
is an isomorphism.

For a group
G
, let
\{G^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
denote the lower central series of
G
, i.e.,
G^{(1)}:=G
and
G^{(i+1)}:=[G^{(i)},G]
. We assume that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{2g}
are generators of
F
and
\lambda
is an element generating
R
as a normal subgroup. Then by
[
28
, Prop.(3.9.13)(i)]
,
\lambda
can be written in the following form

\lambda=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2g}[x_{i},x_{j}]^{a_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime},\quad\lambda^{\prime}\in F^{(3)},\,a_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}.

(3.3)

We let
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{2g}
be the corresponding
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
-basis of
H^{1}(F,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
such that
\chi_{i}(x_{j})=1
if
i=j
and 0 otherwise. Then, by
[
28
, Prop.(3.9.13)(ii)]
, the cup product of
H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
with itself is complelely determined by the
a_{ij}
in (
3.3
): the bilinear form

H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\times H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\overset{\cup}{\longrightarrow}H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\overset{\tr_{\lambda}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}

(3.4)

is given by the matrix
M_{\lambda}=[m_{ij}]
with respect to the basis
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{2g}
, where

m_{ij}=\tr_{\lambda}(\chi_{i}\cup\chi_{j})=\begin{cases}-a_{ij}&\text{ if }i<j\\
a_{ji}&\text{ if }i>j\\
0&\text{ if }i=j.\end{cases}

(3.5)

Since
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
is the pro-
\ell
completion of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})
, it’s clear that
H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
, and we’ve shown in the proof of Lemma
2.2
that the inflation map

H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\longrightarrow H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})

is an isomorphism.
So (
3.4
) implies that the bilinear form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\times H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr_{\lambda}}

(3.6)

is given by the matrix
M_{\lambda}
in (
3.5
).

We consider a
\ell
-Schur covering of
\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t))
(a pro-
\ell
analogue of (
2.8
))

1\longrightarrow\ker\pi_{\ell}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}_{\ell}\overset{\pi_{\ell}}{\longrightarrow}\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t))\longrightarrow 1,

(3.7)

where
\ker\pi_{\ell}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
.
Restricting
\pi_{\ell}
to an extension of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
, we denote
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}:=\pi_{\ell}^{-1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell))
and
\varpi_{\ell}:=\pi_{\ell}|_{\widetilde{H}_{\ell}}
, and obtain the following stem extension of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)

1\longrightarrow\ker\varpi_{\ell}\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}_{\ell}\overset{\varpi_{\ell}}{\longrightarrow}G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)\longrightarrow 1.

(3.8)

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
1\to R\to F\overset{\phi}{\to}G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)\to 1
be a minimal pro-
\ell
presentation of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
. Then there is a surjection
\widetilde{\phi}:F\to\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
such that
\phi=\varpi_{\ell}\circ\widetilde{\phi}
.

(1)

If
\lambda\in R
generates
R
as a normal subgroup of
F
, then
\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda)
is a generator of
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
.
For two elements
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in R
such that each of them generates
R
as a normal subgroup, the followings are equivalent

(a)

\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda_{1})=\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda_{2})

(b)

\lambda_{1}\equiv\lambda_{2}\,\operatorname{mod}\,F^{(3)}
.

(2)

The transgression map in the Hochschild–Serre exact sequence of (
3.8
)

tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}:H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\longrightarrow H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})

is an isomorphism. Moreover, for any class
\varphi\in H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
and any
\lambda\in R
that generates
R
as a normal subgroup, we have
\tr_{\lambda}(\varphi)=(tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}^{-1}\varphi)(\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda))
.

Remark 3.2
.

The surjection
\widetilde{\phi}
is not unique, but the last equality in the statement (
2
) holds for any choice of
\widetilde{\phi}
. Indeed, any two surjections
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{2}
as described in (
1
) differ by a character
\chi:F\to\ker\varpi_{\ell}
whose kernel contains
\lambda
(since
\lambda\in[F,F]
), and hence
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\phi}_{2}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

First, similarly to Lemma
2.10
, the group extension
\varpi_{\ell}
is a stem extension of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
.
Since
F^{ab}\simeq G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)^{ab}
, the following short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow R/[F,R]\longrightarrow F/[F,R]\longrightarrow G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)\longrightarrow 1

(3.9)

is a Schur covering group of
H
(
[
16
, Chap.11, Thm.2.3(iii) and (iv)(b)]
). Moreover, since
H_{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
, the kernel of the Schur covering
R/[F,R]
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
.
Because
\Ext^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)^{ab},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=\Ext^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2g},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=0
,
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
has a unique isomorphism class of Schur covering. So

\varpi_{\ell}
and (
3.9
) are isomorphic as extensions of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
, i.e., there is an isomorphism
\iota
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/[F,R]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(3.10)

commutes. Then we define
\widetilde{\phi}
to be the composition of
F\to F/[F,R]
and
\iota
, and it follows immediately that
\phi=\varpi_{\ell}\circ\widetilde{\phi}
. If
\lambda\in F
generates
R
as a normal subgroup, then
\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda)
generates
\widetilde{\phi}(R)=\ker\varpi_{\ell}
as a normal subgroup of
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
, so it is a generator of
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
, which finishes the proof of the first sentence in (
1
) of the lemma.

Because
R
is contained in
F^{(2)}=[F,F]
, we have
F^{(3)}\supset[F,R]=\ker\widetilde{\phi}
, so (
1a
) implies (
1b
).
Taking the quotient of a group
G
modulo
G^{(i)}
is a functor mapping surjections to surjections. For a quotient map
\pi:E_{1}\twoheadrightarrow E_{2}
, we denote the induced quotient map by
\pi^{(i)}:E_{1}/E_{1}^{(i)}\twoheadrightarrow E_{2}/E_{2}^{(i)}
. So we have a commutative diagram of surjections induced by
\phi
,
\widetilde{\phi}
and
\varpi_{\ell}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/F^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{\ell}/\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)/G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)^{(3)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{(3)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\phi}}^{(3)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{\ell}^{(3)}}

The exact sequence

1\longrightarrow F^{(2)}/F^{(3)}\longrightarrow F/F^{(3)}\longrightarrow F/F^{(2)}\longrightarrow 1

is stem, where
F/F^{(2)}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2g}
and
F^{(2)}/F^{(3)}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2g^{2}-2g}
(because
F^{(2)}/F^{(3)}
is generated by images of
[x_{i},x_{j}]
for
i\neq j
). Because the cup product (
3.4
) for the Demuškin group
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
is non-degenerate, it follows by (
3.3
) that the image of
R
in
F/F^{(3)}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
which is contained in
F^{(2)}/F^{(3)}
.
Recall that
\widetilde{\phi}
factors through the isomorphism
\iota:F/[F,R]\to\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
, the
(F/[F,R])/(F/[F,R])^{(3)}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}/\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{(3)}
, where the former is isomorphic to
F/F^{(3)}
because
[F,R]\subset F^{(3)}
. So we conclude that
\widetilde{\phi}^{(3)}
is isomorphic, and hence the image of
R
in
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}/\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{(3)}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
. Then because the image of
R
in
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
, we see that
\widetilde{\phi}(R)\cap\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{(3)}=1
, so (
1b
) implies (
1a
), and we finish the proof of (
1
).

Because (
3.8
) is a stem extension, in the Hochschild–Serre exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\widetilde{H}_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\overset{tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}}{\longrightarrow}H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}),

the second arrow is an isomorphism, so
tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}
is an injection. On the other hand, because we’ve shown that there is a surjection
\widetilde{\phi}
such that
\phi=\varpi_{\ell}\circ\widetilde{\phi}
, we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(R,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})^{G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}}

where the vertical map is induced by
\widetilde{\phi}|_{R}
.
Moreover, the vertical map is an isomorphism, since
H^{1}(R,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})^{G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)}=\Hom_{F}(R,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=\Hom(R/[F,R],{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
and
\iota
is an isomorphism.
Then from the diagram, we see that
tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}
is an isomorphism and the equality
(tg^{-1}\varphi)(\lambda)=(tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}^{-1}\varphi)(\widetilde{\phi}(\lambda))
holds, which proves the statement (
2
) in the lemma.
∎

Lemma
3.1
shows that, after taking the quotient map
\widetilde{\phi}
, the extension (
3.8
) carries exactly the information of the symplectic form (
3.4
). So we formulate this symplectic form using only the structure of (
3.8
) in the following corollary. Because after taking the quotient map
\widetilde{\phi}
on
F
, the corollary naturally follows by how the minimal presentation (
3.1
) determines the symplectic form (
3.6
), so by abuse of notation, we don’t distinguish the symbols used for
F
and for
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Consider the stem extension (
3.8
), and let
\lambda
be a generator of
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
. Define a trace map
\tr_{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\tr_{\lambda}:H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle(tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}^{-1}\varphi)(\lambda),

where
tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}
is the transgression map in Lemma
3.1
(
2
).
Assume that
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{2g}\}
is a minimal set of generators of
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
. Then
\lambda
can be uniquely written in the form of

\lambda=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2g}[x_{i},x_{j}]^{a_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime},\quad\lambda^{\prime}\in\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{(3)},\,a_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}.

Note that
\{\varpi_{\ell}(x_{1}),\ldots,\varpi_{\ell}(x_{2g})\}
is a minimal set of generators of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
.
Let
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{2g}
be the corresponding
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
-basis of
H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
such that
\chi_{i}(\varpi_{\ell}(x_{j}))=1
if
i=j
and 0 otherwise. Then the symplectic form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\times H^{1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(3.11)

with respect to the basis
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{2g}
is given by the matrix
M_{\lambda}=[m_{ij}]
, where

m_{ij}=\tr_{\lambda}(\chi_{i}\cup\chi_{j})=\begin{cases}-a_{ij}&\text{ if }i<j\\
a_{ji}&\text{ if }i>j\\
0&\text{ if }i=j.\end{cases}

For a positive integer
n
, the symplectic form above with all coefficients replaced with
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell^{n}{\mathbb{Z}}
is given by the matrix
M_{\lambda}
mod
\ell^{n}
.

Proof.

The corollary follows by the argument at the beginning of the section from (
3.1
) to (
3.5
) and Lemma
3.1
.
∎

3.2.
The invariant
\omega_{L/K}
determines the image of Weil pairing

Let
X
be the smooth projective curve over
k
whose function field is
K
, and let
X_{\overline{k}}
denote the base change of
X
to
\overline{k}
.
Let
\Jac_{X}
denote the Jacobian variety of
X_{\overline{k}}
. Then
\Jac_{X}
has an isomorphic principal polarisation, so the Weil pairing associated to
X_{\overline{k}}
is a bilinear pairing

\Jac_{X}[\ell^{n}]\times\Jac_{X}[\ell^{n}]\longrightarrow\mu_{\ell^{n}},

which is non-degenerate and alternating. By
[
27
, Chap.V, §2, Rmk.2.4(f)]
and
[
10
, Dualité §3]
, the Weil pairing can be identified with the cup product of étale cohomology followed by the trace map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{\et}(X_{\overline{k}},\mu_{\ell^{n}})\times H^{1}_{\et}(X_{\overline{k}},\mu_{\ell^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\et}^{2}(X_{\overline{k}},\mu_{\ell^{n}}^{\otimes 2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\ell^{n}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr_{X,\ell^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(3.12)

Since
\mu_{\ell^{n}}
is a trivial
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})
-module, we fix an isomorphism
\mu_{\ell^{n}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell^{n}{\mathbb{Z}}
that is compatible among all
n
; in other words, we pick a generator
\zeta
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
and then identify
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
by sending
1
to
\zeta
. Then it follows by the functorial isomorphism between Galois cohomology and étale cohomology that, for a generator
\lambda
of
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
in (
3.8
), the symplectic form defined by (
3.12
) is equivalent to the one defined by the mod-
\ell^{n}
analogue of (
3.11
) followed by an automorphism of
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell^{n}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Identify
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
with
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
by
1\mapsto\zeta
.

(1)

Let
\lambda
be a generator of
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
in (
3.8
), and let
M_{\lambda}
and
\tr_{\lambda}
be as defined in Corollary
3.3
. The Weil pairing on the
\ell
-adic Tate module of
X_{\overline{k}}

H^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))\times H^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)

is equivalent to the composition of the symplectic form determined by the matrix
M_{\lambda}
and an isomorphism
\tr_{X}\circ\tr_{\lambda}^{-1}\in\Aut({\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
, where
\tr_{X}:=\varprojlim_{n}\tr_{X,\ell^{n}}
.

(2)

Let
\underline{g}:=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
be a system of inertia generators for
K
such that
I(\underline{g})
is the generator
\zeta^{-|\Gamma|}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
. Then let
\lambda
be the image of
g_{1}\cdots g_{m}
in
\ker\varpi_{\ell}
as in the pro-
\ell
analogue of Proposition
2.6
(
2
). Then,
\tr_{\lambda}=\tr_{X}
as trace maps from
H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
.

Proof.

The statement (
1
) follows immediately by taking the inverse limit of (
3.12
) over all
n
.
The statement (
2
) says that the right block of the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tg_{\varpi_{\ell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the trace map
\tr
in the first row is obtained by taking the
(-1)
-Tate twist of the composition map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}_{\et}(X_{\overline{k}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces inf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By taking the inverse limit of the pro-
\ell
analogue of Lemma
2.11
, the homomorphism
[\lambda\mapsto I(\underline{g})]\in H^{1}(\ker\varpi_{\ell},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1))
(which is defined as the homomorphism
\ker\varpi_{\ell}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
that sends
\lambda
to
I(\underline{g})
) is mapped to
-|\Gamma|
in the upper-right entry of the above diagram. By our definition of
\lambda
and
\underline{g}
in the lemma, the preimage of
[\lambda\mapsto I(\underline{g})]
in the lower-left entry is the homomorphism
[\lambda\mapsto-|\Gamma|]
, whose image in the lower-middle entry has
\tr_{\lambda}
exactly
-|\Gamma|
. So we finish the proof.
∎

Next, we will show that the invariant
\omega_{L/K,\ell}
associated to
L/K
carries sufficient information to determine the image of Weil pairing.

Definition 3.5

(The image of the Weil pairing)
.

Assume that
k
contains
\mu_{\ell^{n}}
. Let
L/K
be a subextension of
K^{\#}_{\O}(\ell)/K
such that
L
is Galois over
k(t)
.
Then, by the isomorphism between étale cohomology and Galois cohomology, the Weil pairing defines a symplectic pairing

f_{K,\ell^{n}}:H^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})\times H^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\O}(K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})\to\mu_{\ell^{n}}.

Then the restriction of
f_{K,\ell^{n}}
gives a pairing

f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}:H^{1}(\Gal(L/K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})\times H^{1}(\Gal(L/K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})\longrightarrow\mu_{\ell^{n}},

where the Frobenius
\Frob_{k}
acts trivially on each term as it acts trivially on
\Gal(L/K)
. We call
f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}

the image of the Weil pairing
associated to
L/K
.

For the rest of this section, we assume that
L
and
n
are as described in Definition
3.5
.
Consider compatible
\ell
-Schur coverings of
\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t))
and
\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
(such compatible stem extensions exist by Lemma
2.14
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}

(3.13)

where the rows are
\ell
-Schur coverings, the last vertical map is the quotient of Galois groups, and the first vertical map is the composition of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})(\ell)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})(\ell)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{-1}_{\pi_{\ell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coinf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By restricting
\pi_{\ell}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}
to extensions of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
and
\Gal(L\overline{k}/K\overline{k})\simeq\Gal(L/K)
respectively, we obtain the following compatible extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\varpi_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\varpi^{\prime}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gal(L/K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{\ell}^{\prime}}

(3.14)

where
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}:=\pi_{\ell}^{-1}(G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell))
,
\varpi_{\ell}:=\pi_{\ell}|_{\widetilde{H}_{\ell}}
,
T:={\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}^{-1}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/K\overline{k}))
and
\varpi^{\prime}_{\ell}:=\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}|_{T}
.
These rows are
\Gamma
-equivariant when we choose compatible splittings of
\Gal(K^{\prime}_{\O}(\ell)/\overline{k}(t))\to\Gal(K\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
and
\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))\to\Gal(K\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t))
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Use the notation above. Let
\zeta
be a generator of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
and
\zeta_{n}
the image of
\zeta
in
\mu_{\ell^{n}}
.
Let
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}\}
be a set of generators of
\Gal(L/K)
such that
\Gal(L/K)^{ab}
is the direct products of the images of
\langle y_{i}\rangle
for all
i
. Then there exists a corresponding basis
\{y_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,y_{r}^{\vee}\}
of
H^{1}(\Gal(L/K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})
such that
y_{i}^{\vee}(y_{j})=0
for
i\neq j
.
Also,
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}\}
lifts to a minimal set of generators
\{\widetilde{y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}_{r}\}
of
T
in (
3.14
).

Let
\lambda_{L}
denote the image of
\zeta^{-|\Gamma|}
under the following composition map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\Gal(L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)),{\mathbb{Z}})(\ell)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}=\ker\varpi^{\prime}_{\ell},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{L/K,\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}^{-1}}

where
\omega_{L/K,\ell}
is the invariant defined in Remark
2.16
(
3
) and
d_{\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}}
is the differential map defined by
\pi^{\prime}_{\ell}
. Then
\lambda_{L}
can be expressed in the form of

\lambda_{L}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}[\widetilde{y}_{i},\widetilde{y}_{j}]^{b_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime}_{L},\quad\lambda^{\prime}_{L}\in T^{(3)},\,b_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}.

With respect to the basis
y_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,y_{r}^{\vee}
, the image of the Weil pairing
f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}
is given by

f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}(y_{i}^{\vee},y_{j}^{\vee})=\begin{cases}\left(y_{i}^{\vee}(y_{i})\cdot y_{j}^{\vee}(y_{j})\right)^{-b_{ij}}&\text{ if }i<j\\
\left(y_{i}^{\vee}(y_{i})\cdot y_{j}^{\vee}(y_{j})\right)^{b_{ji}}&\text{ if }i>j\\
0&\text{ if }i=j.\end{cases}

Proof.

There exist elements
t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}
of
\Gal(L/K)^{ab}
such that
\Gal(L/K)^{ab}=\langle t_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\langle t_{r}\rangle
. So there exists a basis
\{y_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,y_{r}^{\vee}\}
of
H^{1}(\Gal(L/K),\mu_{\ell^{n}})=H^{1}(\Gal(L/K)^{ab},\mu_{\ell^{n}})
such that
y_{i}^{\vee}(t_{j})=0
for
i\neq j
. We pick
y_{i}\in\Gal(L/K)
to be a lift of
t_{i}
. By the Burnside basis theorem,
y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}
form a minimal set of generators of
\Gal(L/K)
. Since
T
is a stem extension of
\Gal(L/K)
, we have
T^{ab}\simeq\Gal(L/K)^{ab}
and hence
\{\widetilde{y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}_{r}\}
is a minimal set of generators of
T
.

Let
\lambda
be as defined in Lemma
3.4
(
2
). So
\lambda_{L}
is
\alpha(\lambda)
, where
\alpha
is the middle vertical arrow in (
3.14
). We let
M_{\lambda}
,
x_{i}
,
\chi_{i}
,
m_{ij}
and
a_{ij}
be as defined in Corollary
3.3
for
1\leq i,j\leq 2g
, then let
\chi_{i}[n]
denote the composition of
\chi_{i}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}\to\mu_{\ell^{n}}
(defined by
1\mapsto\zeta_{n}
).
Let
D=[d_{ij}]
be a
2g\times r

{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
-entry matrix such that

\alpha(x_{i})\equiv\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\widetilde{y}_{j})^{d_{ij}}\mod T^{(2)},

and let
E=[e_{ij}]
be a
2g\times r

{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
-entry matrix such that

y_{j}^{\vee}=\sum_{i=1}^{2g}e_{ij}\chi_{i}[n].

We compute
y_{j}^{\vee}(\alpha(x_{i}))
by viewing
y_{j}^{\vee}
as a homomorphism from
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
to
\mu_{\ell^{n}}
that factors through
\Gal(L/K)
. On one hand, we have

y_{j}^{\vee}(\alpha(x_{i}))=y_{j}^{\vee}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{r}(\widetilde{y}_{s})^{d_{is}}\right)=y_{j}^{\vee}(y_{j})^{d_{ij}};

and on the other hand,

y_{j}^{\vee}(\alpha(x_{i}))=y_{j}^{\vee}(x_{i})=\prod_{s=1}^{2g}\chi_{s}[n](x_{i})^{e_{sj}}=(\zeta_{n})^{e_{ij}}.

So we see that
E=D\Delta
, where
\Delta
is the
r\times r
diagonal matrix whose
(i,i)
-entry is
\log_{\zeta_{n}}y_{i}^{\vee}(y_{i})
. By
\lambda_{L}=\alpha(\lambda)
and Corollary
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2g}[\alpha(x_{i}),\alpha(x_{j})]^{a_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime}\quad\quad\text{ for some }\lambda^{\prime}\in T^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2g}\left[\prod_{s=1}^{r}(\widetilde{y}_{s})^{d_{is}},\prod_{t=1}^{r}(\widetilde{y}_{t})^{d_{jt}}\right]^{a_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime\prime}\quad\quad\text{ for some }\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in T^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq s<t\leq r}[\widetilde{y}_{s},\widetilde{y}_{t}]^{-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq 2g}d_{is}d_{jt}m_{ij}}\cdot\lambda^{\prime\prime}

So we see that
-b_{st}
is the
(s,t)
-entry of
D^{T}M_{\lambda}D
for
1\leq s<t\leq r
.
Then by Corollary
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
(
2
)

f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}\left(y_{i}^{\vee},y_{j}^{\vee}\right)=f_{L/K,\ell^{n}}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{2g}e_{si}\chi_{s}[n],\sum_{t=1}^{2g}e_{tj}\chi_{t}[n]\right)=(\zeta_{n})^{(i,j)\text{-entry of }E^{T}M_{\lambda}E},

which proves the proposition.
∎

4.
The function field moments as
q\to\infty

In this section, we study the relationship between the
\omega
-invariant
\omega_{L/K}
and the EVW-W lifting invariant. Building on the strategy of the work
[
21
]
of Wood, Zureick-Brown and the author, we give the proof of Theorem
1.1
by counting rational points on Hurwitz schemes with prescribed
\omega
-invaraint.

4.1.
Revisit: Hurwitz schemes and a lifting invariant of Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland and Wood

In
[
37
]
, Wood defined and proved basic properties of a lifting invariant of covers of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
over an algebraically closed field, which was introduced by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland.
Let
G
be a finite group and
c
a subset of
G
closed under conjugation by elements of
G
and closed under invertible powering. Let
c/G
denote the set of conjugacy classes of elements in
c
.

Let
U(G,c)
be the group defined by presentation with generators
[g]
for
g\in c
, and relations
[x][y][x]^{-1}=[xyx^{-1}]
for
x,y\in c
. Then there is a natural surjection
U(G,c)\to G
mapping
[g]\mapsto g
and a natural surjection
U(G,c)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
mapping
[g]
to a generator for the conjugacy class of
g
. These two surjections induce the same map
U(G,c)\to G^{ab}
to the abelianization of
G
, so we obtain a homomorphism
U(G,c)\to G\times_{G^{ab}}{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
. This homomorphism is actually a surjection, and
[
37
, Thm.2.5]
proves that
U(G,c)
is isomorphic to the fiber product
S_{c}\times_{G^{ab}}{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
, where
S_{c}
is a reduced Schur covering for
G
and
c
defined as follows.
A
reduced Schur covering

S_{c}\to G
is defined to be the quotient of a Schur covering
\phi:S\to G
by the normal subgroup generated by the set of commutators

\{[\hat{x},\hat{y}]\mid\hat{x},\hat{y}\in S,\,\phi(\hat{x})\in c,\,\text{and }[\phi(\hat{x}),\phi(\hat{y})]=1\}.

Via the isomorphic differential map
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})\to\ker\phi
, the kernel of a reduced Schur covering is isomorphic to a quotient of
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})
, which we denote by
H_{2}(G,c)
; and one can prove by definition that
H_{2}(G,c)
does not depend on the choice of the Schur covering that we start with. Thus, the kernel of the natural quotient map
U(G,c)\to G
is isomorphic to
H_{2}(G,c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
, and we denote this kernel by
K(G,c)
.

Let
p
be a prime not dividing
|G|
.
There is an action of
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{(p)^{\prime}}^{\times}
on the set of elements of
K(G,c)
. Let
S_{c}\to G
be a reduced Schur covering for
G
and
c
. Then elements of
K(G,c)
can be written in the form
(g,\underline{m})
for
g\in\ker(S_{c}\to G)
and
\underline{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
. For each conjugacy class
\gamma\in c/G
, we pick an element
x_{\gamma}
in
\gamma
and a lift
\widehat{x_{\gamma}}
of
x_{\gamma}
in
S_{c}
. Then for each
\alpha\in\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{(p)^{\prime}}^{\times}
and
\gamma\in c/G
, we define

w_{\alpha}(\gamma)=\widehat{x_{\gamma}}^{-\alpha}\widehat{x_{\gamma}^{\alpha}}\in\ker(S_{c}\to G)
, where
\widehat{x_{\gamma}^{\alpha}}
is the picked lift of the element in the conjugacy class of
\gamma^{\alpha}
.
Then we define a group homomorphism

W_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}\to\ker(S_{c}\to G)

by sending the generators for the conjugacy class
\gamma
to
w_{\alpha}(\gamma)
.
It was shown in
[
37
, §4]
that
W_{\alpha}
does not depend on the choice of the lifts
\widehat{x_{\gamma}}
. Then, for each
\alpha\in\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{(p)^{\prime}}^{\times}
, we define an action of
\alpha
on the set
K(G,c)
by

\alpha\ast(g,\underline{m})=(g^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}(\underline{m}),\underline{m}^{\alpha}),

(4.1)

where
g^{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
th power of
g
and
\underline{m}^{\alpha}
is a rearranging of the coordinates of
\underline{m}
by sending the coordinate of
\gamma
to the coordinate of
\gamma^{\alpha}
. This action of
\alpha
is not a group homomorphism.

Let
\overline{k}
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
such that
p\nmid|G|
if
p\neq 0
(here we include the case
p=0
). A branched
G
-cover
X
of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
over
\overline{k}
that is ramified at
\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{m}\}
defines a surjection

\varphi:\pi_{1}^{\et}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\overline{k}}-\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}\})_{(p)^{\prime}}\longrightarrow G.

Assume that all the inertia groups of the cover are generated by elements in
c
. We let
\underline{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
be a system of inertia generators for
\pi_{1}^{\et}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\overline{k}}-\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}\})_{(p)^{\prime}}
and let
I(\underline{g})
is the corresponding generator of
\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p)^{\prime}}
. Then
EVW-W lifting invariant
associated to this cover
X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\overline{k}}
is defined to be the
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}
-equivariant map of sets

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{z}}:\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle K(G,c)

\displaystyle I(\underline{g})

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[\varphi(g_{1})]\cdots[\varphi(g_{m})].

There is a Hurwitz scheme
\HHur^{n}_{G,c}
defined over
{\mathbb{Z}}[|G|^{-1}]
, whose points represent tame Galois covers
X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with
n
branch points (i.e., the degree of each geometric fiber of
D\to\Spec({\mathbb{Z}}[|G|^{-1}])
is
n
, where
D\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is the branch loci of the cover
X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
) together with a choice of identification
\Gal(X/{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\simeq G
and a choice of point over infinity, such that all inertia subgroups are generated by elements in
c
and the place at
\infty
is unramified (see
[
21
, §11]
for more details about this Hurwitz scheme). For each geometric point
\overline{s}
on
\HHur^{n}_{G,c}
whose residue field has characteristic
p
, we can attach to it the lifting invariant
{\mathfrak{z}}_{\overline{s}}
as defined above. The lifting invariant is actually a component invariant: for any algebraically closed field
k
, the
k
-points of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c})_{k}
in the same component have the same lifting invariant (
[
21
, Cor.12.2]
).
Moreover, when
M
is sufficiently large, there is a bijection between the set

{\mathcal{K}}:=\{(g,\underline{m})\in K(G,c)\mid\text{coordinates of $\underline{m}$ are at least $M$ and sum up to $n$}\}
and
the components of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
(and similarly, the components of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
) whose lifting invariant has image in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
In particular, by
[
21
, Thm.12.1]
, if
\overline{s}
is a
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
-point of
(\HHur_{G,c}^{n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
, then for a
\zeta\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)^{\times}_{(q)^{\prime}}
, we have

{\mathfrak{z}}_{\Frob(\overline{s})}(\zeta)=q^{-1}\ast{\mathfrak{z}}_{\overline{s}}(\zeta),

(4.2)

where
\Frob
is the Frobenius map
\Frob_{(\HHur_{G,c}^{n})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}}
and the
\ast
operator is as defined in (
4.1
).

In order to compute the number of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-points of
\HHur_{G,c}^{n}
acting on
(\HHur_{G,c}^{n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
, by the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula, it is crucial to understand the number of
\Frob_{(\HHur_{G,c}^{n})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}}
-fixed components of
(\HHur_{G,c}^{n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
, which we denote by
\pi_{G,c}(q,n)
.
For integers
n
and
M
, we write
{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}_{\equiv q,n,\geq M}
for the subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
consisting of elements satisfying: 1) the coordinates are constant on each set of conjugacy classes of
c/G
that can be obtained from one another by taking
q
th powers, 2) the coordinates sum up to
n
, and 3) each coordinate is at least
M
. Then there is a homomorphism
{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}_{\equiv q,n,\geq M}\to G^{ab}
that is the restriction of the natural map
{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}\to G^{ab}
.
Then by
[
21
, Prop.12.7]
, the number of
\Frob_{(\HHur^{n}_{G,c})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}}
-fixed components of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
has the following estimation

\pi_{G,c}(q,n)=b(G,c,q,n)+O_{G}(n^{d_{G,c}(q)-2}),

(4.3)

where
d_{G,c}(q)
is the number of orbits of
q
th powering on the conjugacy classes in
c/G
and

b(G,c,q,n):=\sum_{h\in\ker(S_{c}\to G)}\#\left\{\underline{m}\in\ker\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}_{\equiv q,n,\geq 0}\to G^{ab}\right)\,\biggr\rvert\,W_{q^{-1}}(\underline{m})^{q}=h^{q-1}\right\}.

The definition of
b(G,c,q,n)
above appears differently from but agrees with the one in
[
21
]
(see the proof of
[
21
, Prop.12.7]
).

4.2.
Relationship between the EVW-W lifting invariant and the invariant
\omega_{L/K}

In this subsection, we will prove in Proposition
4.3
that the
\omega
-invariant
\omega_{L/K}
equals the prime-to-
|\Gamma|
-torsion part the EVW-W lifting invariant.
Throughout this subsection, we consider the EVW-W lifting invariants when
G
is a semidirect product
H\rtimes\Gamma
with
\gcd(|H|,|\Gamma|)=1
and
c
is the set consisting of elements of
G\backslash\{1\}
that have the same order as their image in
\Gamma
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite group and
H
a
|\Gamma|^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-group. Suppose that there is a stem extension of groups

1\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}\rtimes\Gamma\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}H\rtimes\Gamma\longrightarrow 1,

such that
A
has order prime to
|\Gamma|
. Let
x
and
y
be two elements of
H\rtimes\Gamma
such that
[x,y]=1
and
x
has order equal to the order of its image in
\Gamma
. Then
[\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y}]=1
for any lifts
\widetilde{x}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
and
\widetilde{y}\in\pi^{-1}(y)
.

Proof.

We let
\gamma
denote the image of
\widetilde{x}
in
\Gamma
. By the Schur–Zassenhaus theorem, we can pick a homomorphic splitting
s:\Gamma\to\widetilde{H}\rtimes\Gamma
such that
s(\gamma)=\widetilde{x}
. We can redefine the group
\widetilde{H}\rtimes\Gamma
as the semidirect product
\widetilde{H}\rtimes s(\Gamma)
, and then using the notation of elements in semidirect product, we write
\widetilde{x}
as
(1,\gamma)
and write
\widetilde{y}
as
(b,\sigma)
for
b\in\widetilde{H}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
. So
[\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y}]=\widetilde{x}^{-1}\widetilde{y}^{-1}\widetilde{x}\widetilde{y}=(\gamma^{-1}\circ\sigma^{-1}(b^{-1}\gamma(b)),[\gamma,\sigma])
. Since
[x,y]=1
, we have that
[\gamma,\sigma]=1
and
b^{-1}\gamma(b)\in\ker\pi
, and it’s left to show that
b^{-1}\gamma(b)=1
.

We consider the action of the subgroup
\langle\gamma\rangle
of
\Gamma
on the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow 1.

For a
\langle\gamma\rangle
-group
G
, we denote
Y_{\gamma}(G):=\left\{g^{-1}\gamma(g)\mid g\in G\right\}
. It follows by
[
21
, Lem.3.5 and Lem.3.6]
that
|A|=|A^{\langle\gamma\rangle}||Y_{\gamma}(A)|
,
|Y_{\gamma}(A)||Y_{\gamma}(H)|=|Y_{\gamma}(\widetilde{H})|
, and the elements of
Y_{\gamma}(\widetilde{H})
are equidistributed in
Y_{\gamma}(H)
. Thus, because
\Gamma
acts trivially on
A
, we see that
A^{\langle\gamma\rangle}=A
, so
Y_{\gamma}(A)=1
and therefore
Y_{\gamma}(\widetilde{H})\cap A=1
. Then it follows that if
b^{-1}\gamma(b)\in\ker\pi
for some
b\in\widetilde{H}
, then
b^{-1}\gamma(b)=1
, which finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
p
be a prime,
\Gamma
a finite group, and
H
a finite admissible
\Gamma
-group such that
p\nmid|H||\Gamma|
. Let
c_{\Gamma}
be the set
\Gamma\backslash\{1\}
. Let
G:=H\rtimes\Gamma
and
c_{G}
the set of elements of
G\backslash\{1\}
that have the same order as their image in
\Gamma
.

(1)

The order of the kernel of the quotient map
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{2}(G,c_{G})
has order supported on the set of prime divisors of
|\Gamma|
.

(2)

The quotient map
G\to\Gamma
induces a surjection
H_{2}(G,c_{G})\to H_{2}(\Gamma,c_{\Gamma})
, and the kernel of this surjection has order prime to
|\Gamma|
.

In particular, combining the two statements above, we have

H_{2}(G,c_{G})\simeq H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\oplus H_{2}(\Gamma,c_{\Gamma}).

(4.4)

Proof.

Let
\pi:S\to G
be a Schur covering of
G
, and
\pi_{c_{G}}:S_{c_{G}}\to G
its reduced Schur covering associated
c_{G}
. By taking quotient of
S
modulo the
|\Gamma|
-part of
\ker\pi
, we have a stem extension

1\longrightarrow(\ker\pi)_{(|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1.

(4.5)

Then by Lemma
4.1
, the image of
\ker(S\to S_{c_{G}})
in
S^{\prime}
is trivial, so
\pi_{c_{G}}
factors through
S^{\prime}
, which proves the statement (
1
).

By the proof of
[
21
, Lem.12.10]
, there are compatible Schur coverings of
G
and
\Gamma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma,}

where the left vertical surjection is obtained by taking quotient of
S
by the prime-to-
|\Gamma|
part of
\ker(S\to G)
. By definition of
c_{G}
and
c_{\Gamma}
, one can check that the diagram above defines the following compatible reduced Schur coverings, where the kernel of the left vertical map has order prime to
|\Gamma|
, which proves the statement (
2
) in the proposition.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{c_{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\Gamma}_{c_{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.}

Finally, by a basic fact that a Schur covering group of
\Gamma
(resp.
G
) has order supported on prime divisors of
|\Gamma|
(resp.
|G|
), we have that
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
is the prime-to-
|\Gamma|
completion of
H_{2}(G,c_{G})
and
H_{2}(\Gamma,c_{\Gamma})
is the
|\Gamma|
-completion of
H_{2}(G,c_{G})
, so the equality
4.4
follows.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
k
,
K
,
p
,
\Gamma
and
L
be as described in Definition
2.13
. Let
G
denote the Galois group of
L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)
, and
c
the set of elements of
G\backslash\{1\}
that have the same order as their image in
\Gamma
(where the quotient map
G\to\Gamma
is defined by the tower of fields
L\overline{k}/K\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)
).
Then the composition map

\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}\overset{{\mathfrak{z}}}{\longrightarrow}K(G,c)\longrightarrow H_{2}(G,c)\longrightarrow H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}

extends to the
\omega
-invariant
\omega_{L/K}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\to H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
associated to
L/K
.
Here
{\mathfrak{z}}
is the EVW-W lifting invariant associated to
L\overline{k}/\overline{k}(t)
, the second map is the projection of from
K(G,c)=H_{2}(G,c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
to the first coordinate, and the last map is the prime-to-
(p|\Gamma|)
completion map, which is well-defined by Lemma
4.2
(
1
).

Proof.

Let
\pi:S\to G
be a Schur covering, and
\pi_{c}:S_{c}\to G
its reduced Schur covering associated to
c
. Let
S^{\prime}\to G
be as defined in (
4.5
).
The elements in
c
have order dividing
|\Gamma|
.
For each conjugacy class in
c/G
, we pick one element
x
in this class, and then pick one preimage
\widehat{x}
of
x
in
S_{c}
such that the image of
\widehat{x}
in
S^{\prime}
is the unique preimage of
x
whose order equals
\ord(x)
. For any other element
y=g^{-1}xg
in this conjugacy class, we define
\widehat{y}:=\tilde{g}^{-1}\widehat{x}\tilde{g}
for some preimage
\tilde{g}
of
g
in
S_{c}
(by
[
37
, Lem.2.3]
,
\widehat{y}
is independent of the choice of
\tilde{g}
).
By
[
37
, Thm.2.5]
, there is an isomorphism from
U(G,c)
to
S_{c}\times_{G^{ab}}{\mathbb{Z}}^{c/G}
defined by mapping the generator
[x]
for each
x\in c
to
(\widehat{x},e_{x})
, where
e_{x}
is the generator corresponding to the conjugacy class of
x
.

Let
\underline{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
be a system of inertia generators associated to
K
, and
\varphi
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
denote the surjections, defined in Corollary
2.15
, that map from
\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\rangle
to
G
and
S^{\prime}
respectively. Then one can check that the composition map

\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}\overset{{\mathfrak{z}}}{\longrightarrow}K(G,c)\longrightarrow\ker(S_{c}\to G)\longrightarrow\ker(S^{\prime}\to G)

(4.6)

sends
I(\underline{g})
to
\varphi(g_{1}\cdots g_{m})
. We will show in the next paragraph that this composition map extends to a group homomorphism
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p)^{\prime}}\to\ker(S^{\prime}\to G)
, although
{\mathfrak{z}}
is only a
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{(p)^{\prime}}^{\times}
-equivariant map of sets.
Then the proposition follows by composing the map (
4.6
) with the inverse of the differential map
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\ker(S^{\prime}\to G)
.

For each
x\in c
, we let
\widetilde{x}
denote the unique preimage of
x
in
S^{\prime}
such that
\ord(\widetilde{x})=\ord(x)
. Recall that the lift
\widehat{x}
of
x
in
S_{c}
that we’ve picked has image
\widetilde{x}
in
S^{\prime}
. So for each
\alpha\in\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\times}_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
, we have
\widetilde{x^{\alpha}}=\widetilde{x}^{\alpha}
, and hence
\widehat{x}^{-\alpha}\widehat{x^{\alpha}}
is contained in
\ker(S_{c}\to S^{\prime})
for any
x\in c
. So for any element
(g,\underline{m})\in K(G,c)
(using notation in (
4.1
)), the image of
\alpha\ast(g,\underline{m})
under the map
K(G,c)\to\ker(S^{\prime}\to G)
equals the
\alpha
th power of the image of
(g,\underline{m})
. So we proved that (
4.6
) can extend to a group homomorphism
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p)^{\prime}}\to\ker(S^{\prime}\to G)
.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Throughout this subsection, we assume that
\Gamma
is a finite group and
H
is a finite admissible
\Gamma
-group. We define
G:=H\rtimes\Gamma
, let
c_{2}
denote the set
\Gamma\backslash\{1\}
, and
c_{1}
the set of elements of
G\backslash\{1\}
that have the same order as their image in
\Gamma
. Let
q
be a power of a prime number
p
such that
p\nmid|G|
.

By Lemma
4.2
, there exists a commutative diagram of surjections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{S}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{S}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{2}}

(4.7)

where
\pi^{1}
is a reduced Schur covering of
G
and
c_{1}
,
\pi^{2}
is a reduced Schur covering of
\Gamma
and
c_{2}
, and
\pi^{\prime}
is a
(q|\Gamma|)^{\prime}
-Schur covering of
G
.
Moreover, since
\gcd\left(|\ker\rho\circ\pi^{\prime}|,|\ker\pi^{2}|\right)=1
,
\overline{S}^{1}
is the fiber product
S^{\prime}\times_{\Gamma}\overline{S}^{2}
.

For a positive integer
n
and a group homomorphism
\delta:\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(q|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\to\ker\pi^{\prime}
, we let
\pi_{G,c_{1}}^{\delta}(q,n)
denote the number of
\Frob_{(\HHur_{G,c_{1}}^{n})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}}
-fixed components of
(\HHur_{G,c_{1}}^{n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
whose corresponding
\omega
-invariant is the composition of
\delta
with the inverse of the differential map of
\pi^{\prime}
. We pick a
\zeta\in\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(q|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}^{\times}
and let
\eta:=\delta(\zeta)
. Define the quantity
b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta)
as follows, which is independent of the choice of
\zeta
.

b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\ker\pi^{1}\\
\text{s.t. }\phi(h)=\eta\end{subarray}}\#\left\{\underline{m}\in\ker\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}_{\equiv q,n,\geq 0}\to G^{ab}\right)\,\biggr\rvert\,W_{q^{-1}}(\underline{m})^{q}=h^{q-1}\right\}

Lemma 4.4
.

Given an element
a\in({\mathbb{Z}}/|G|^{2}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}
, there is a positive integer
M_{a}
and a non-empty set
E_{a}
of residues modulo
M_{a}
, and positive numbers
r^{\prime}_{a,b}
for
b\in E_{a}
, such that for a prime power
q\equiv a\,(\operatorname{mod}|G|^{2})
and a non-negative integer
n
the following holds.

(1)

If
\ord(\eta)\mid q-1
and
n\,(\operatorname{mod}M_{a})=b\in E_{a}
, then

\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,n)=b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta)+O_{G}(n^{d_{G,c_{1}}(q)-2})=r^{\prime}_{a,b}n^{d_{G,c_{1}}(q)-1}+O_{G}(n^{d_{G,c_{1}}(q)-2}).

(2)

If
\ord(\eta)\nmid q-1
or
n\,(\operatorname{mod}M_{a})\not\in E_{a}
, then
\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,n)=b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta)=0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
and
[
21
, Cor.12.6]
,
\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,n)
can be approximated by the number of
\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}
-equivariant maps of sets
{\mathfrak{z}}:\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)^{\times}_{(p)^{\prime}}\to K(G,c_{1})
such that
{\mathfrak{z}}
reduces to
\delta
and all coordinates of the image of
{\mathfrak{z}}
compositiond with the projection
K(G,c_{1})\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}
is at least
M
for a sufficiently large
M
. Then the lemma follows from the proof of Proposition 12.7 and Corollary 12.9 of
[
21
]
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For any group homomorphism
\delta:\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}\to\ker\pi^{\prime}
such that
\ord(\im\delta)\mid q-1
, we have

b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta)=b(\Gamma,c_{2},q,n).

Proof.

By definition of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, the quotient map
\rho:G\to\Gamma
sends each element
c_{1}
to an element in
c_{2}
. Moreover, as
\gcd(|H|,|\Gamma|)=1
, by the Schur–Zassenhaus theorem, for each element of
c_{2}
, all of its lifts in
c_{1}
are conjugate to each other, thus there is a natural bijective correspondence
{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{2}/\Gamma}
induced by
\rho
. Since
H
is an admissible
\Gamma
-group, we have
G^{ab}=\Gamma^{ab}
, so the restriction of this bijection gives a natural isomorphism
\ker({\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}\to G^{ab})\simeq\ker({\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{2}/\Gamma}\to\Gamma^{ab})
.

We write the group homomorphisms
W^{1}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}\to\ker\pi_{1}
and
W^{2}_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{2}/\Gamma}\to\ker\pi_{2}
for
W_{\alpha}
(defined above the equation (
4.1
)) associated to
(G,c_{1})
and
(\Gamma,c_{2})
respectively. For each
g\in c_{1}/G
and its image
\gamma\in c_{2}/\Gamma
, we can pick
x_{g}
in
g
and
x_{\gamma}
in
\gamma
such that
\rho(x_{g})=x_{\gamma}
, and then pick their lifts
\widehat{x_{g}}\in\overline{S}^{1}
and
\widehat{x_{\gamma}}\in\overline{S}^{2}
such that
\widetilde{\rho}(\widehat{x_{g}})=\widehat{x_{\gamma}}
. Then by definition of
W^{1}_{\alpha}
and
W^{2}_{\alpha}
, we have the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\overline{S}^{1}\overset{\pi^{1}}{\to}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{2}/\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\overline{S}^{2}\overset{\pi^{2}}{\to}\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{1}_{q^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{2}_{q^{-1}}}

Pick a
\zeta\in\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)^{\times}_{(p|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}
and let
\eta:=\delta(\zeta)
. Considering the diagram (
4.7
) and the isomorphism
\overline{S}^{1}\simeq S^{\prime}\times_{\Gamma}\overline{S}^{2}
, we obtain the following identity of sets

\displaystyle\left\{h\in\ker(\overline{S}^{1}\overset{\pi^{1}}{\to}G)\,\bigr\rvert\,\phi(h)=\eta\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{1-1}{\longleftrightarrow}

\displaystyle\ker(\overline{S}^{2}\overset{\pi^{2}}{\to}\Gamma)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(h).

Therefore, combining everything, we have

\displaystyle b(G,c_{1},q,n;\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\ker(\overline{S}^{1}\to G)\\
\text{s.t. }\phi(h)=\eta\end{subarray}}\#\left\{\underline{m}\in\ker\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{1}/G}_{\equiv q,n,\geq 0}\to G^{ab}\right)\,\biggr\rvert\,W^{1}_{q^{-1}}(\underline{m})^{q}=h^{q-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{h}\in\ker(\overline{S}^{2}\to\Gamma)}\#\left\{\underline{m}\in\ker\left({\mathbb{Z}}^{c_{2}/\Gamma}_{\equiv q,n,\geq 0}\to\Gamma^{ab}\right)\,\biggr\rvert\,W^{2}_{q^{-1}}(\underline{m})^{q}=\bar{h}^{q-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(\Gamma,c_{2},q,n).

∎

By the correspondence between smooth projective curves and their function fields,
\HHur^{n}_{G,c_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
parametrizes the pairs
(L,\iota)
, where
L
is an extension of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t)
that is split completely at
\infty
and has
\rDisc L=q^{n}
, and
\iota
is an isomorphism
\Gal(L/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t))\overset{\sim}{\to}G
such that each inertia subgroup of
L/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t)
is generated by an element in
c_{1}
. We fix a surjection
\rho:G\to\Gamma
as defined in the diagram (
4.7
). Then for each pair
(L,\iota)
, consider the subfield
K:=L^{\ker(\rho\circ\iota)}
of
L
. The surjection
\rho\circ\iota
defines an isomorphism
\Gal(K/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t))\to\Gamma
, and moreover
L/K
is unramified and
\rDisc(K)=q^{n}
.
Then the restriction of
\iota
to
\Gal(L/K)
gives an isomorphism
\Gal(L/K)\to H
.

Lemma 4.6
.

There is a surjective map of sets

\displaystyle\HHur_{G,c_{1}}^{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\coprod_{K\in E_{\Gamma}(q^{n},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t))}\Sur_{\Gamma}(G^{\#}_{\O}(K),H)

(4.8)

\displaystyle(L,\iota)

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle\{G^{\#}_{\O}(K)\to\Gal(L/K)\}.

Each element in the set on the right has
[H:H^{\Gamma}]
preimages.

Proof.

The lemma follows by the proofs of Lemmas 9.3 and 10.2 in
[
21
]
.
∎

Let
q
be a prime power such that
\gcd(q,|G|)=1
and
\ord(\im\delta)\mid q-1
. Then taking
q
th power on
\im\delta
is the identity map. So by the Frobenius action on lifting invariants in (
4.2
) and Proposition
4.3
, if there is a
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
-point of a connected component of
(\HHur_{G,c_{1}}^{n})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
has
\omega
-invariant
\delta
, then all the
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
-point of this component have
\omega
-invariant
\delta
.
So we let
Z^{\delta}_{q,n}
be the union of components of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c_{1}})_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
whose
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
-points has
\omega
-invariant
\delta
.
By Lemma
4.6
, we have

\frac{\#Z^{\delta}_{q,n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}{[H:H^{\Gamma}]}=\sum_{K\in E_{\Gamma}(q^{n},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t))}\#\left\{\pi\in\Sur_{\Gamma}\left(G^{\#}_{\O}(K),H\right)\,\bigl\rvert\,\pi^{*}\circ\omega^{\#}_{K}=\delta\right\}.

By the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula, since
(Z^{\delta}_{q,n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}}
is
n
-dimensional (
[
21
, Lem.10.3]
), we have

\#Z^{\delta}_{q,n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})=\sum_{j=0}^{2n}(-1)^{j}\Tr(\Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}|H^{j}_{c,\et}((Z^{\delta}_{q,n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}},{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}))

where
H^{j}_{c,\et}
is the
j
th étale fundamental group with compact support.
Here
\Tr(\Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}|H^{j}_{c,\et}((Z^{\delta}_{q,n})_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}},{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}))
is
\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,n)q^{n}
when
j=2n
, and is at most
q^{j/2}C(n)
when
0\leq j<2n
by the comparison of étale and analytic cohomology of components of the Hurwitz scheme
[
21
, Lem.10.3]
. Here

C(n)=\max_{0\leq j<2n}\dim H^{2n-j}(Z^{\delta}_{{\mathbb{C}},n},{\mathbb{Q}}),

where
Z^{\delta}_{{\mathbb{C}},n}
is the union of components of
(\HHur^{n}_{G,c_{1}})_{{\mathbb{C}}}
whose prime-to-
|\Gamma|
-torsion part of the EVW-W lifting invariant

\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)^{\times}\overset{{\mathfrak{z}}}{\longrightarrow}K(G,c_{1})\longrightarrow H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})_{(|\Gamma|)^{\prime}}

is given by
\delta
.
So we round down
N
to the nearest integer whose residue mod
M_{a}
is in
E_{a}
, and so we have

\sum_{n\leq N}\#Z^{\delta}_{q,n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})=\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,N)q^{N}+O_{N,G}(q^{N-1/2}).

Similarly,
E_{\Gamma}(q^{n},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(t))=\#\HHur_{\Gamma,c_{2}}^{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, and

\sum_{n\leq N}\#\HHur^{n}_{G,c_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})=\pi_{\Gamma,c_{2}}(q,N)q^{N}+O_{N,\Gamma}(q^{N-1/2}).

Therefore, the fraction in the limits of the left-hand side of the equality in Theorem
1.1
equals

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[H:H^{\Gamma}]}\frac{\sum_{n\leq N}\#Z^{\delta}_{q,n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}{\sum_{n\leq N}\#\HHur^{n}_{\Gamma,c_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[H:H^{\Gamma}]}\frac{\pi^{\delta}_{G,c_{1}}(q,N)q^{N}+O_{N,G}(q^{N-1/2})}{\pi_{\Gamma,c_{2}}(q,N)q^{N}+O_{N,\Gamma}(q^{N-1/2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[H:H^{\Gamma}]}\left(1+\frac{O_{G}(N^{d_{\Gamma}(q)-2})+O_{N,G}(q^{-1/2})}{\pi_{\Gamma,c_{2}}(q,N)+O_{N,\Gamma}(q^{-1/2})}\right),

where the last equality follows by Lemmas
4.4
,
4.5
and
[
21
, Prop.12.7]
. Then the equality in Theorem
1.1
follows after taking limits.

5.
A random pro-
\ell

\Gamma
-group for
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}

In this section, we assume that
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\ell
is an odd prime that is not
\Char(k)
. We construct a random group to model the distribution of
G^{\#}_{\O}(K)(\ell)
as
K
varies among all the quadratic extensions of
k(t)
that is split completely at
\infty
.

Recall that
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
is a pro-
\ell
Demuškin group whose abelianization is free of rank
2g
, where
g
is the genus of the curve over
\overline{k}
corresponding to
K\overline{k}
. We’ve shown that
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
has a
\Gamma
action and a
\Frob_{k}
action, and it admits a
\Gamma
-equivariant and
\Frob_{k}
-equivariant stem extension (
3.8
) whose kernel is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}(1)
. Moreover,
G^{\#}_{\O}(K)(\ell)
is the maximal
\Gamma
-equivariant and
\Frob_{k}
-equivariant quotient of
G_{\O}(K\overline{k})(\ell)
whose induced
\Frob_{k}
-action is trivial. So our strategy is to treat the
\Frob_{k}
action as a random automorphism of the stem extension. We start with a Demuškin group with desired properties, pick a random automorphism that acts on the kernel of the stem extension as taking the
|k|
-th powers. Then define the random group to be the maximal quotient fixed by this random automorphism, as the generating rank of the Demuškin group goes to infinity.

5.1.
Construction of the random group

By the classification of Demuškin groups
[
28
, Thm.(3.9.11)]
, if a Demuškin group has abelianization isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2n}
, then it is isomorphic to
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n}\mid[x_{1},x_{2}][x_{3},x_{4}]\cdots[x_{2n-1},x_{2n}]\rangle
. In the following lemma, we show that there is a unique (up to isomorphism)
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on this type of Demuškin group such that the Demuškin group is an admissible
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-group.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
G
be a pro-
\ell
group and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Aut(G)
have the same order which is prime to
\ell
. Assume that
\pi:G\to H
is a surjection such that
\ker\pi\subset[G,G]
, and that
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
preserve
\ker\pi
and induce the same automorphism on
H
. Then there exists
\tau\in\Aut(G)
such that
\sigma_{1}=\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\tau
and
\tau
preserves
\ker\pi
and induces the trivial action on
H
.

Proof.

Consider the subgroup
\Aut(G,\pi)
of
\Aut(G)
consisting of all the automorphisms of
G
preserving
\ker\pi
. There is a natural map
\alpha:\Aut(G,\pi)\to\Aut(H)
. Since
\ker\pi\subset[G,G]
, by
[
15
, Chap.5, Thm.3.5]
, the kernel of
\alpha
is a pro-
\ell
group. The assumptions in the lemma imply that
\alpha(\sigma_{1})
equals
\alpha(\sigma_{2})
and has order prime to
\ell
. Then by the Schur–Zassenhaus theorem,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are conjugate in
\Aut(G,\pi)
(and hence in
\Aut(G)
) by an element in
\ker\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
a Demuškin group whose abelianization is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2n}
. Then
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
admits a Schur covering (unique up to isomorphism)

1\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}\longrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\overset{\pi_{n}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\longrightarrow 1.

There exists a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
action on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
such that the action preserves and acts trivially on
\ker\pi_{n}
and its induced action on
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{ab}
is taking inversion. Moreover, if
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are two such
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
actions on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
, then there exists an automorphism
\tau
of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
such that
\sigma_{1}=\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\tau
, and
\tau
preserves
[\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n},\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}]
and induces the trivial action on
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{ab}
.

Proof.

Consider the pro-
\ell
group defined by the following presentation

G:=\langle x_{1},\cdots,x_{2n}\mid x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{2n}\rangle.

(5.1)

Let
F
denote the free pro-
\ell
generated by
x_{1},\cdots,x_{2n}
, and let
R
denote the closed normal subgroup of
F
generated by
x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n}
.
Note that

x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n}=[x_{1},x_{2}][x_{1}x_{2},x_{2n}\cdots x_{3}]x_{3}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{3}\cdots x_{2n},

\text{and }[x_{1}x_{2},x_{2n}\cdots x_{3}]\equiv[x_{1},x_{3}]\cdots[x_{1},x_{2n}][x_{2},x_{3}]\cdots[x_{2},x_{2n}]\mod F^{(3)}.

By repeating this process, we have
x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n}\equiv\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2n}[x_{i},x_{j}]\mod F^{(3)}
. So by
[
28
, Prop.3.9.13(ii)]
, the cup product
H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\times H^{1}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})\to H^{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})
is non-degenerate. Moreover,
G^{ab}
is free of rank
2n
. So
G
is a Demuškin group whose abelianization is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{2n}
and hence is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, and we will use this presentation (
5.1
) of
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
in the rest of the proof.

The existence and uniqueness of the Schur covering
\pi_{n}
follow immediately from
H_{2}(G,{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
and
\Ext_{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{1}(G^{ab},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell})=0
. We define
\sigma\in\Aut(F)
by
\sigma(x_{i})=x_{i}^{-1}
. We will show next that
\sigma
induces a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
action on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
as described in the lemma. First, because

\sigma(x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n})=x_{1}\cdots x_{2n}x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}=(x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n})^{x_{2n}^{-1}\cdots x_{1}^{-1}},

(5.2)

the automorphism
\sigma
stabilizes
R
. So
\sigma
induces an automorphism
\overline{\sigma}
of
G
, and
\overline{\sigma}
acts on
G^{ab}
by inversion. The presentation of
G
defines a stem extension of
G
with kernel isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}

1\longrightarrow R/[F,R]\longrightarrow F/[F,R]\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1,

where the image of
x_{1}^{-1}\cdots x_{2n}^{-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{2n}
generates the kernel
R/[F,R]
.
So (
5.2
) shows that
\sigma
preserves
[F,R]
, and hence it induces an automorphism of
F/[F,R]
, and moreover this automorphism acts trivially on
R/[F,R]
. So we proved the existence of the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
action on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
as described in the lemma.

Suppose that
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are two
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
actions on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
as described in the lemma.
Then since they induce the same automorphism on
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{ab}
, the last statement of the lemma follows by Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Definition 5.3

(Definition of the random group)
.

Let
n
be a positive integer,
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
,
\pi_{n}
and the
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
action on them be as defined in Lemma
5.2
, and
q
be a positive integer such that
\ell\nmid q
. We fix a generator
\xi_{n}
of
\ker\pi_{n}
.
Let
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma,\pi_{n};q)
denote the set of automorphisms of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma
that preserves and acts as multiplication by
q
on
\ker\pi_{n}
.
Let
\phi
be a random element of
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma,\pi_{n};q)
with respect to the “Haar” measure. We let
1-\phi
denote the quotient map from
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma
to its maximal
\Gamma
-equivariant quotient on which the induced
\phi
action is trivial.
Then we define the random group
X_{q,n}
as

X_{q,n}:=(1-\phi)(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma)/(1-\phi)(\ker\pi_{n}).

(5.3)

Moreover, we fix a homomorphic splitting of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma\to\Gamma
and hence an action of
\Gamma
(by conjugation) on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}
. Then
\ker(X_{q,n}\to\Gamma)
naturally obtains an action of
\Gamma
. We define the following random
\Gamma
-group

Y_{q,n}:=\ker(X_{q,n}\to\Gamma).

Remark 5.4
.

(1)

We need to explain what the “Haar” measure is in the definition. Since
\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma
is a finitely generated profinite group,
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma)
is profinite by
[
29
, Cor.4.4.4]
. The subgroup
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma,\pi_{n};1)
consisting of all automorphisms that stabilizes and acts trivially on
\ker\pi_{n}
is a closed subgroup of
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma)
, so it is a profinite group, and hence has a Haar measure. Then since
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma,\pi_{n};q)
is a coset of
\Aut(\widetilde{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}\rtimes\Gamma,\pi_{n};1)
in
